Discrete mathematics , 229(1-3):371–410, 2001. C.12 COUNTING 4-CRITICAL LINEAR TRIPLE SYSTEMS 

R¨ odl and Siggers conjectured that the number of k-critical (r, l )-systems on n vertices is exponential in nl. We disprove this for (k, r, l ) = (4 , 3, 2) : there is a constant c > 0 such that for infinitely many n there are at least exp( cn 2 log n) pairwise nonisomorphic 4-critical linear triple systems on 

n vertices. The construction lifts a dense 4-critical graph to a triple system along a proper edge coloring of that graph, in such a way that the edge coloring can be read off from any minimal non-

3-colorable subsystem of the lift. The extra log n in the exponent is exactly the entropy of the edge coloring, and it survives the passage from labeled systems to isomorphism classes. C.12.1 INTRODUCTION 

A hypergraph H is k-colorable if its vertices can be colored with k colors so that no edge is monochromatic, and k-chromatic if k is the least such number. Following R¨ odl & Siggers (2006), 

H is k-critical if it is k-chromatic, has no isolated vertices, and H − e is (k − 1) -colorable for every edge e of H. An (r, l )-system is an r-uniform hypergraph in which no l-set of vertices lies in more than one edge; a (3 , 2) -system is a linear triple system . Write T (k, r, l, n ) for the number of nonisomorphic k-critical (r, l )-systems on n vertices. The first bound on this count is due to Abbott et al. (1980), who showed that for all k, r ≥ 3 there is a constant b = b(k, r ) > 1 with T (k, r, 2, n ) > b n for all large n. R¨ odl & Siggers (2006) proved that for all k ≥ 3 and r > l ≥ 2 and all large n there is a k-critical (r, l )-system on n vertices with at least c0 nl edges, which is optimal up to the constant since an (r, l )-system on n vertices has at most  nl

  rl

 edges. Feeding that density into a construction indexed by the bipartitions of the edge set, they improved the Abbott–Liu–Toft bound to 

T (k, r, l, n ) > α nl

for all k ≥ 3, r > l ≥ 2 and all large n, with a constant α = α(k, r, l ) > 1 (R¨ odl & Siggers, 2006, Theorem 6.1). Every system they produce has at least c′nl edges for a constant c′ > 0, and Section 6 of R¨ odl & Siggers (2006) closes with a trivial upper bound and a conjecture: 79 Preprint 

A trivial upper bound for the number of (r, l )-systems with c′nl edges is  (nr )

> c′nl

 <   nr

> c′nl

 ≈

(nr−l)nl

= O(d nl log nr−l

). We conjecture that the actual number is in fact exponential in nl.

We read “the actual number” as T (k, r, l, n ), which the preceding theorem bounds from below by 

α nl

; the content of the conjecture is then the matching upper bound T (k, r, l, n ) ≤ Cnl

for some constant C = C(k, r, l ), that is, that the factor log nr−l in the trivial bound is an artifact of the counting. For graphs the corresponding assertion is immediate, since there are only 2(n

> 2

) graphs on n labeled vertices. Once r > l , the trivial count of (r, l )-systems with Θ( nl) edges carries a logarithm in the exponent, and the conjecture asserts that criticality removes it. We show that criticality does not remove it, already for linear triple systems and k = 4 .On the competing reading, in which “the actual number” counts all (r, l )-systems on n vertices with 

Ω( nl) edges, criticality plays no role and the logarithm is present for a direct counting reason, so it is the reading above that is at issue. Beyond the lower bound α nl

of R¨ odl & Siggers (2006) we are aware of no further work on the growth of T (k, r, l, n ), and the problem is not recorded in the survey of color-critical hypergraphs of Kostochka (2006), which concerns critical systems with few edges rather than their number. 

Theorem C.12.1. There is a constant c > 0 and there are infinitely many integers n such that 

T (4 , 3, 2, n ) ≥ exp  c n 2 log n.

In particular there is no constant C for which T (4 , 3, 2, n ) ≤ Cn2

for all n.

We now summarize the construction. Fix a 4-critical graph G on m vertices with Ω( m2) edges, which exists by a theorem of Toft, and fix a palette [t] with t = 4 m. Every proper edge coloring 

β : E(G) → [t] is turned into a linear triple system Jβ on a vertex set that does not depend on β:take three copies v1, v 2, v 3 of each vertex v of G together with one palette vertex wj for each j ∈ [t],and place over each edge uv of G the three triples {ui, v i, w β(uv )}. Two forcing gadgets of R¨ odl & Siggers (2006) are then attached: one chains the palette vertices so that they all receive a common color, and one ties v1, v 2, v 3 together so that they receive three distinct colors. A proper 3-coloring of Jβ would therefore select, for each v, the unique copy vi carrying the palette color, and v 7 → i

would be a proper 3-coloring of G. The key point is that a minimal non-3-colorable subsystem Kβ

of Jβ must retain a triple over every edge of G, since G is 4-critical; that triple exhibits the value 

β(uv ), so β 7 → Kβ is injective on labeled systems. There are exp(Ω( m2 log m)) choices of β, while 

Jβ has only O(m) vertices, so passing to isomorphism classes costs a factor exp( O(m log m)) and the bound survives. C.12.2 THE CONSTRUCTION 

We use two known ingredients. The first is a theorem of Toft (1970): there is a constant a > 0 such that for every sufficiently large m there is a 4-critical graph on m vertices with at least a m 2 edges. As recorded by R¨ odl & Siggers (2006), one may take a = 1 /16 here. The second is the pair of forcing gadgets of R¨ odl & Siggers (2006). We recall only the properties used below. 

Lemma C.12.2 (Forcing gadgets) . There are fixed finite linear triple systems S and D with the following properties. 1. S has two designated vertices x, y , and no edge of S contains both of them. In every proper 

3-coloring of S the vertices x, y receive the same color, and conversely every assignment of a common color to x and y extends to a proper 3-coloring of S.2. D has three designated vertices u1, u 2, u 3. In every proper 3-coloring of D these three vertices receive three distinct colors, and conversely every assignment of three distinct colors to u1, u 2, u 3 extends to a proper 3-coloring of D.Proof. Take S = S(3 , 3) and D = D(3 , 3) from R¨ odl & Siggers (2006, Construction 3.1). Both are 3-chromatic (3 , 2) -systems, so each has at least one proper 3-coloring, and the two forward 80 Preprint implications are exactly the forcing properties for which those gadgets are built. That no edge of 

S contains both designated vertices is noted in the proof of that construction: S(3 , 3) is obtained by deleting edges from the system left when one edge through x and y is removed from a 4-critical linear triple system, and such a system exists by Abbott & Liu (1978, Theorem 1). For the converse statements, fix a proper 3-coloring ψ0 of S. Then ψ0(x) = ψ0(y), and given any target color γ we may compose ψ0 with a permutation of [3] carrying ψ0(x) to γ. Likewise fix a proper 3-coloring ψ0 of D; then ψ0(u1), ψ 0(u2), ψ 0(u3) are distinct, and given distinct target colors 

γ1, γ 2, γ 3 the assignment ψ0(ui) 7 → γi is a well-defined permutation of [3] , with which we compose 

ψ0.Fix from now on an integer m large enough for Toft’s theorem, and let G be a 4-critical graph on m

vertices with 

e(G) ≥ a m 2.

Set t = 4 m, and let A be the set of proper edge colorings β : E(G) → [t], that is, of maps assigning distinct colors to any two edges sharing an endpoint. 

Lemma C.12.3. |A| ≥ (2 m)e(G).Proof. Order E(G) arbitrarily and color greedily. When the edge uv is reached, the colors already used on edges meeting uv number at most 

(deg u − 1) + (deg v − 1) ≤ 2m − 4,

since every degree is at most m − 1. As t = 4 m, at least 2m + 4 colors are available at every step. Distinct sequences of choices produce distinct colorings, since the edge order is fixed, so 

|A| ≥ (2 m)e(G).We now define the ambient vertex set. Let 

V ∗ = {v1, v 2, v 3 : v ∈ V (G)} ∪ { w1, . . . , w t} ∪ W, 

where W consists of t − 1 disjoint sets of fresh internal vertices, one for a copy of S attached to each consecutive pair wj , w j+1 , together with m further disjoint sets of fresh internal vertices, one for a copy of D attached to each triple v1, v 2, v 3. The set V ∗ does not depend on β.For β ∈ A let Jβ be the hypergraph on V ∗ whose edges are the following. For every uv ∈ E(G)

and every i ∈ [3] there is the lifted triple 

{ui, v i, w β(uv )}. (33) For every j ∈ [t − 1] there are the edges of a copy of S whose designated vertices are identified with wj and wj+1 and whose remaining vertices are the corresponding fresh set in W . For every 

v ∈ V (G) there are the edges of a copy of D whose designated vertices are identified with v1, v 2, v 3

and whose remaining vertices are the corresponding fresh set in W . Figure 13 shows the three pieces. 

Lemma C.12.4. For every β ∈ A the hypergraph Jβ is a linear triple system, and |V ∗| ≤ C0m for a constant C0 depending only on S and D.Proof. Write s = |V (S)| and d0 = |V (D)|. Counting the vertices of V ∗ layer by layer, 

|V ∗| = 3 m + t + ( t − 1)( s − 2) + m(d0 − 3) ≤ (4 s + d0 − 4) m, 

since t = 4 m; take C0 = 4 s + d0 − 4.Every edge of Jβ has three vertices, so it remains to check that no pair of vertices lies in two edges. Consider first a pair of the form {ui, v i} with u̸ = v. No edge of a gadget copy contains copies of two distinct vertices of G, and a lifted triple containing both ui and vi lies over the edge uv in layer 

i, so at most one edge contains the pair. Next consider a pair {vi, w j }. Gadget copies contribute no such edge, and a lifted triple containing this pair lies over an edge of G incident to v and colored 

j; since β is proper there is at most one such edge, and the layer i is determined as well. A pair 

{wj , w j′ } lies in no lifted triple, and it lies in no gadget edge either, because the copies of S meet each other only in single palette vertices and no edge of S contains both designated vertices. A pair 81 Preprint

On the upper right, the chain of copies of S that forces w1, . . . , w t to receive a single common color. } with i̸ = i′ lies in no lifted triple, and the only gadget copy containing it is the copy of D

attached to v. A pair {ui, v i′

Every remaining pair contains a fresh internal vertex, which lies in a single gadget copy, so every edge containing the pair is an edge of that copy; each copy is itself a linear triple system, so there is at most one such edge. Hence no pair of vertices lies in two edges of Jβ .

Lemma C.12.5. For every β ∈ A the hypergraph Jβ is not 3-colorable. Proof. Suppose ψ is a proper 3-coloring of Jβ . The copies of S give ψ(wj ) = ψ(wj+1 ) for every 

j ∈ [t − 1] by Lemma C.12.2, so 

ψ(w1) = ψ(w2) = · · · = ψ(wt),

and after relabeling the colors we may assume this common value is 1. For each v ∈ V (G) the copy of D attached to v1, v 2, v 3 forces those three vertices to receive three distinct colors, so there is a unique index ϕ(v) ∈ [3] with ψ(vϕ(v)) = 1 .Let uv ∈ E(G) and suppose ϕ(u) = ϕ(v) = i. Then the lifted triple {ui, v i, w β(uv )} is monochro-matic of color 1, a contradiction. Hence ϕ is a proper 3-coloring of G, which is impossible because 

G is 4-chromatic. C.12.3 RECOVERING THE EDGE COLORING 

For uv ∈ E(G) write 

Luv = {ui, v i, w β(uv )} : i ∈ [3] 

for the set of three lifted triples over uv . These sets are pairwise disjoint as uv ranges over E(G).

Lemma C.12.6. Let K ⊆ Jβ be a subhypergraph that is not 3-colorable. Then K contains a triple from Luv for every uv ∈ E(G).Proof. Suppose instead that K contains no triple of Luv for some uv ∈ E(G), so that K is a subhypergraph of Jβ − Luv . We exhibit a proper 3-coloring of Jβ − Luv , which restricts to one of 

K. Since G is 4-critical, G − uv has a proper 3-coloring ϕ : V (G) → [3] . Give every palette vertex 

wj the color 1, and for each z ∈ V (G) color z1, z 2, z 3 with the three colors in such a way that zϕ(z)

receives the color 1.Let {xi, y i, w β(xy )} be a retained lifted triple, so xy ∈ E(G) and xy ̸ = uv . If this triple were monochromatic then, as wβ(xy ) has color 1, we would have ϕ(x) = ϕ(y) = i, contradicting ϕ(x)̸ =

82 Preprint 

ϕ(y). So no retained lifted triple is monochromatic. On each copy of S the two designated vertices have received the same color, and on each copy of D the three designated vertices have received three distinct colors, so by Lemma C.12.2 the coloring extends over the fresh internal vertices of every gadget copy. Thus Jβ − Luv is 3-colorable, contradicting the hypothesis on K.For each β ∈ A choose an edge-minimal non-3-colorable subhypergraph of Jβ and delete its isolated vertices; call the result Kβ . Such a subhypergraph exists by Lemma C.12.5. 

Lemma C.12.7. Each Kβ is a 4-critical linear triple system with at least e(G) edges. Moreover the map β 7 → Kβ is injective. Proof. Linearity is inherited from Jβ by Lemma C.12.4. By the choice of Kβ it is not 3-colorable while Kβ − e is 3-colorable for every edge e, and it has no isolated vertices. It is 4-colorable: delete an edge e, take a proper 3-coloring of Kβ − e, and if e is monochromatic recolor one vertex of e

with a fourth color. The recolored vertex then lies in no monochromatic edge, since it is the only vertex of that color, and every other edge is unaffected. Hence Kβ is 4-chromatic and 4-critical. By Lemma C.12.6 it contains a triple of Luv for each of the e(G) edges uv of G, and these sets are pairwise disjoint, so Kβ has at least e(G) edges. For injectivity, fix uv ∈ E(G) and read off β(uv ) from the labeled hypergraph Kβ as follows. By Lemma C.12.6 some triple of the form {ui, v i, w j } belongs to Kβ , and as observed in the proof of Lemma C.12.4 the only edges of Jβ containing ui and vi are the lifted triples over uv , all of which use the palette vertex wβ(uv ). So j = β(uv ). Therefore Kβ determines β, and distinct edge colorings give distinct labeled hypergraphs on V ∗.C.12.4 PROOF OF THE MAIN THEOREM

Journal of Graph Theory ,53(1):56–74, 2006. Bjarne Toft. On the maximal number of edges of critical k-chromatic graphs. (No Title) , 1970. C.13 STRONGLY CONNECTED DIGRAPHS WITH NO SMALL k-KERNEL 

This problem asks how small a k-kernel a strongly connected digraph is guaranteed to have. Spiro asked whether |V (G)|/(k +1)+ Ok(1) is always enough once k ≥ 3; we show that it is not, and that the coefficient 1/(k + 1) is itself wrong, the truth lying between 1/k and 1/(k − 1) . The digraphs witnessing this are a hub feeding m parallel directed paths that all return through one common tail, long enough to force the hub out of every k-kernel. Two refutations of the same question were published after our run, and the construction below was obtained independently of both. C.13.1 INTRODUCTION 

All digraphs here are finite and have no loops or parallel arcs; directed cycles of length two are allowed. A set X ⊆ V (G) is stable if no arc of G has both ends in X, and for an integer k ≥ 1

a k-kernel of G is a stable set X ⊆ V (G) such that every vertex of G can be joined from X by a directed path of length at most k. Paths of length 0 are allowed, so a k-kernel covers itself. A 1-kernel is a kernel and a 2-kernel is a quasikernel. In a tournament every stable set is a single vertex, so a quasikernel is exactly a king (Post & Zheng, 2023). We write κk(G) for the minimum size of a 

k-kernel of G.Not every digraph has a kernel, but every digraph has a quasikernel by the theorem of Chv´ atal & Lov´ asz (2006), so κk(G) is defined for every k ≥ 2. How small a quasikernel one is entitled to expect is the subject of the small quasi-kernel conjecture , that κ2(G) ≤ | V (G)|/2 whenever G has no source. It was posed by P. L. Erd˝ os and Sz´ ekely in 1976 and stated in print by Erd˝ os & Sz´ ekely (2010); Erd˝ os et al. (2023) survey what is known. After reversing every arc, k-kernels become the 

(2 , k )-kernels of Kwa´ snik (2006), that is, the stable sets that absorb every vertex within distance k.Spiro (2026) initiated their extremal study and asked what the source-free hypothesis buys when it is strengthened to strong connectivity. 

If D is a strongly connected digraph and q ≥ 3 is an integer, does there exist a q-kernel Q of D such that |Q| ≤ |V (D)| 

> q+1

+ Oq (1) ?

This is Question 7.7 of Spiro (2026), and it is recorded as Conjecture 1.4 by Nguyen et al. (2024), who attribute it to Spiro and leave it open. We write k throughout for the integer called q there. Spiro observes that the coefficient 1/(k + 1) would be best possible, by considering a collection of directed cycles Ck+2 all sharing a single vertex. The restriction to k ≥ 3 is necessary, and is what distinguishes the published form of the question from the first preprint version, which asked it for k ≥ 2: Example 17 of Erd˝ os et al. (2023) exhibits strongly connected digraphs whose smallest quasikernel has size ( 12 − o(1)) |V (G)|, which is far above |V (G)|/3.On the positive side, Spiro (2026) showed that under the hypotheses of the question there is always a k-kernel of size at most about |V (G)|/ log k, by producing about log k pairwise disjoint k-kernels. 84 Preprint This was improved by Nguyen et al. (2024, Theorem 1.5), who proved that every digraph G with 

|V (G)| > 1 admitting a spanning out-arborescence, and in particular every strongly connected digraph, satisfies 

κk(G) ≤ 1 + |V (G)| − 2

k − 1 (k ≥ 2) . (34) They deduce equation 34 from the acyclic case, where the coefficient improves to 1/k : an acyclic digraph on at least two vertices with only one source has a k-kernel of size at most 1 + ( |V (G)| − 

2) /k . The digraph of their Figure 1, a source y with a single arc to a hub x together with m

disjoint directed paths on k vertices leaving x, shows that this is tight. We show that the answer to Question 7.7 is negative for every k ≥ 3, and that it fails by a linear rather than a constant margin. 

Theorem C.13.1. For every integer k ≥ 2 and every integer m ≥ 1 there is a strongly connected digraph Gk,m on mk + k + 2 vertices whose smallest k-kernel has size exactly 

κk(Gk,m ) = m + 1 = |V (Gk,m )| − 2

k .

Consequently, for every k ≥ 2 and every constant c there is a strongly connected digraph G with 

κk(G) > |V (G)|/(k + 1) + c.

Theorem C.13.1 does more than defeat the additive term Ok(1) : it shows that the coefficient 1/(k +1) is itself wrong. Write c∗ 

> k

for the infimum of the constants c for which κk(G) ≤ c|V (G)| + Ok(1) 

holds for all strongly connected G. Theorem C.13.1 gives c∗ 

> k

≥ 1/k and equation 34 gives c∗ 

> k

≤

1/(k − 1) . For k ≥ 3, whether the value 1/k is itself admissible is asked by Wang et al. (2026); for 

k = 2 the corresponding statement c∗ 

> 2

= 1 /2 would follow from the small quasi-kernel conjecture, since every strongly connected digraph is source-free. The excess is already substantial for moderate parameters: κ3(G3,50 ) = 51 on 155 vertices against 155 /4 = 38 .75 , and κ10 (G10 ,50 ) = 51 on 

512 vertices against 512 /11 ≈ 46 .55 . Taking k = 2 gives strongly connected digraphs whose smallest quasikernel has size exactly 12 |V (G)| − 1, one less than the bound of the small quasi-kernel conjecture; digraphs achieving this up to o(|V (G)|) were already given by Erd˝ os et al. (2023). Two refutations have since appeared. Wang et al. (2026) give for each k ≥ 3 strongly connected di-graphs every k-kernel of which has size at least (|V (G)|− 2) /k ; this is the bound of Theorem C.13.1, which also covers k = 2 . Penev et al. (2026) obtain the weaker bound κk(G) > (1 /k − ε)|V (G)|,but with the extra feature that the digraphs may be taken of arbitrarily large directed girth. By con-trast, every directed cycle of Gk,m passes through the hub x and hence traverses a whole arm, so these cycles have exactly the lengths k + 2 , k + 3 , . . . , 2k + 2 and Gk,m has directed girth only 

k + 2 . The same paper shows that for odd k ≥ 3 every source-free bipartite digraph with a spanning out-arborescence, and in particular every strongly connected bipartite digraph, has a k-kernel of size at most |V (G)|/(k + 1) + 1 . For odd k, then, no bipartite digraph can refute Question 7.7, and indeed the lengths just listed include odd ones, so Gk,m is not bipartite. The construction is the acyclic example of Figure 1 of Nguyen et al. (2024) with a return path attached: the m parallel directed paths leaving the hub x, each on k vertices, are brought back to x

through one common tail. In the acyclic example the hub is unusable because the source y lies in every k-kernel and sends an arc to x; here y is no longer a source, and the tail takes over that role. The tail is long enough that some vertex of it must be used to cover its own far end, and every vertex of the tail sends an arc to the hub; this forces the hub out of every k-kernel and thereby forces each of the m paths to pay for itself. C.13.2 THE CONSTRUCTION 

Fix integers k ≥ 2 and m ≥ 1. The digraph Gk,m has vertex set 

V (Gk,m ) = {x, y, p 1, . . . , p k} ∪ { ai,j : 1 ≤ i ≤ m, 1 ≤ j ≤ k}

and arc set consisting of 

y → x, pj → x (1 ≤ j ≤ k), pj → pj+1 (1 ≤ j < k ), pk → y, 

together with, for each i ∈ { 1, . . . , m }, the arm 

x → ai, 1 → ai, 2 → · · · → ai,k → p1.

> xa1,1a1,2

· · · a1,k 

...  

> am, 1am, 2

· · · am,k 

> p1p2

· · · pky

Figure 14: The digraph Gk,m . The hub x feeds m arms, each a directed path with k internal vertices running from x to p1, and the tail p1 → p2 → · · · → pk → y → x closes the digraph up. The dashed arcs pj → x, present for every j, are what keep x out of every k-kernel. There are no other arcs. Write Ai := {ai, 1, . . . , a i,k } for the ith arm and P := {y, p 1, . . . , p k} for the tail . Thus V (Gk,m ) is the disjoint union of {x}, P and A1, . . . , A m, and 

|V (Gk,m )| = 1 + ( k + 1) + mk = mk + k + 2 . (35) Figure 14 shows the digraph. 

Lemma C.13.2. Gk,m is strongly connected. Proof. Every vertex reaches x: the vertex y and each pj do so along a single arc, and ai,j reaches 

p1 along its own arm and then uses p1 → x. Conversely x reaches every vertex: it reaches all of Ai

along the ith arm and then p1, then p2, . . . , p k along the tail, and finally y. Hence any vertex reaches any other through x.C.13.3 A SMALL k-KERNEL 

We first exhibit a k-kernel of the size claimed in Theorem C.13.1. 

Lemma C.13.3. The set K0 := {y} ∪ { ai,k : 1 ≤ i ≤ m} is a k-kernel of Gk,m , and |K0| = m + 1 .Proof. First, K0 is stable. Indeed the only arcs incident with y are y → x and pk → y, and the only arcs incident with ai,k are ai,k −1 → ai,k and ai,k → p1; none of x, pk, p1, ai,k −1 lies in K0. In particular distinct vertices ai,k and ai′,k are nonadjacent, as they lie on different arms. For the covering condition, y → x gives dist( y, x ) = 1 , and y → x → ai, 1 → · · · → ai,j is a directed path of length j + 1 , so dist( y, a i,j ) ≤ k for every j ≤ k − 1. Each ai,k covers itself, and 

ai,k → p1 → p2 → · · · → pj has length j ≤ k, so any one ai,k covers all of p1, . . . , p k. Every vertex of Gk,m is therefore joined from K0 by a directed path of length at most k.C.13.4 EVERY k-KERNEL IS LARGE 

We now show that no k-kernel of Gk,m can be smaller. 

Lemma C.13.4. Every k-kernel K of Gk,m satisfies |K| ≥ m + 1 .Proof. The crucial observation is that the far end of the tail and the far end of each arm can each be covered from only one place. First consider y. The unique in-neighbor of y is pk, the unique in-neighbor of pj is pj−1 for 2 ≤

j ≤ k, and the in-neighbors of p1 are the m vertices ai,k . Walking backwards from y therefore gives 86 Preprint 

dist( pk+1 −ℓ, y ) = ℓ for 1 ≤ ℓ ≤ k, while every remaining vertex is at distance at least k + 1 from 

y. So the vertices u with dist( u, y ) ≤ k are exactly the k + 1 vertices of P , and hence K ∩ P̸ = ∅.Next, every vertex of P has an arc to x, by y → x and pj → x. Picking u ∈ K ∩ P , the pair u, x is joined by an arc, so stability of K forces x / ∈ K.Now fix i and consider ai,k . The unique in-neighbor of ai,j is ai,j −1 for 2 ≤ j ≤ k, and the unique in-neighbor of ai, 1 is x. Walking backwards from ai,k therefore gives dist( ai,k −ℓ, a i,k ) = ℓ for 

1 ≤ ℓ ≤ k − 1 and dist( x, a i,k ) = k, while every remaining vertex is at distance at least k + 1 from 

ai,k . So the vertices u with dist( u, a i,k ) ≤ k are exactly the k + 1 vertices of Ai ∪ { x}, and since 

x / ∈ K we get K ∩ Ai̸ = ∅.The sets P, A 1, . . . , A m are pairwise disjoint, so |K| ≥ m + 1 .C.13.5 PROOF OF THE MAIN THEOREM 

Proof of Theorem C.13.1. The digraph Gk,m is strongly connected by Lemma C.13.2 and has mk +

k + 2 vertices by equation 35. Lemmas C.13.3 and C.13.4 give κk(Gk,m ) = m + 1 , and 

m + 1 = mk + kk = |V (Gk,m )| − 2

k

by equation 35. For the last assertion, fix k ≥ 2 and compute 

κk(Gk,m ) − |V (Gk,m )|

k + 1 = ( m + 1) − mk + k + 2 

k + 1 = (m + 1)( k + 1) − mk − k − 2

k + 1 = m − 1

k + 1 .

This tends to infinity as m → ∞ , so given c we may choose m with (m − 1) /(k + 1) > c and take 

G = Gk,m .

Remark C.13.5 . The hub-and-arms mechanism is not tied to the particular tail used above. Fix inte-gers k ≥ 2 and t ≥ 1, and write C = {c0, . . . , c k}, R = {r1, . . . , r k−1} and Bi = {bi, 1, . . . , b i,k }

for 1 ≤ i ≤ t. The digraph Hk,t has vertex set 

V (Hk,t ) = C ∪ { x, z } ∪ R ∪ B1 ∪ · · · ∪ Bt

and arc set consisting of 

c0 → c1 → · · · → ck → c0, cj → x (0 ≤ j ≤ k), z → r1 → · · · → rk−1 → c0,

together with, for each i ∈ { 1, . . . , t }, the arm 

x → bi, 1 → · · · → bi,k → z. 

Then Hk,t is strongly connected on kt + 2 k + 2 vertices, and κk(Hk,t ) = t + 1 . The upper bound comes from the k-kernel {c0} ∪ { bi,k : 1 ≤ i ≤ t}, and the lower bound repeats the proof of Lemma C.13.4. Indeed the only arc entering C from outside is rk−1 → c0, so a path from outside C

to ck has length at least 1 + dist( c0, c k) = k + 1 and ck is covered only from within C; every cj has an arc to x, so x lies in no k-kernel; and the only arc entering Bi from outside is x → bi, 1, so bi,k is covered only from Bi ∪{ x} and every k-kernel meets every Bi. Since t+1 = ( |V (Hk,t )|− k−2) /k ,this family gives the same coefficient 1/k as Theorem C.13.1 but loses 1 in the additive constant.

C.14 F -POSITIVITY OF CHROMATIC SYMMETRIC FUNCTIONS OF HYPERTREES 

The chromatic symmetric function of a hypergraph is the generating function for the colorings under which no hyperedge is monochromatic. Unlike its graph analogue, it need not expand nonnegatively in Gessel’s fundamental quasisymmetric basis, but Taylor (2015) proved that it does whenever the hypergraph is a hypertree all of whose hyperedges have prime size, and conjectured that primality is superfluous. We prove the conjecture. The prime case rests on partitioning the nonconstant colorings of an r-element hyperedge into r! blocks indexed by the linear orders of that hyperedge, and no general construction of such a partition is known. We replace the partition by a decomposition of the indicator function of “nonconstant” into chain conditions with nonnegative rational weights; such a decomposition exists for every r, and it glues across a hypertree exactly as a partition would. C.14.1 INTRODUCTION 

A hypergraph is a pair H = ( V, E ) with V finite and E a family of subsets of V , each of size at least 2, called hyperedges . A coloring of H is a map κ : V → N, where N = {1, 2, . . . }, and κ is 

proper if no hyperedge is monochromatic, that is, if κ is nonconstant on every e ∈ E. The chromatic symmetric function of H is 

XH = X 

> κproper

Y

> v∈V

xκ(v),

introduced for ordinary graphs by Stanley (1995) and extended to hypergraphs by Stanley (1998). Write n = |V | and [n] = {1, . . . , n }. For S ⊆ [n − 1] the fundamental quasisymmetric function of Gessel (1984) is 

F (n) 

> S

= X 

> i1≤···≤ in
> j∈S⇒ij<i j+1

xi1 · · · xin ,

and the F (n) 

> S

with S ⊆ [n − 1] form a basis of the degree-n component of the ring QSym of quasisymmetric functions. A homogeneous quasisymmetric function of degree n is F -positive if all of its coefficients in this basis are nonnegative. A path in H is a sequence v1, e 1, v 2, e 2, . . . , e m, v m+1 with vi, v i+1 ∈ ei for each i, in which the hyperedges ei are distinct and the vertices vi are distinct except that v1 = vm+1 is allowed. It is a 

cycle if v1 = vm+1 and m ≥ 2. The hypergraph H is connected if any two vertices are joined by a path, and a hypertree is a connected hypergraph with no cycles; this is the convention of Gessel & Kalikow (2005) adopted by Taylor (2015). For an ordinary graph, XG is always F -positive: Stanley (1995) splits the proper colorings ac-cording to the acyclic orientation each one induces and identifies every piece as a (P, ω )-partition enumerator. For hypergraphs this fails. Taylor (2015) records that the hypergraph on V = [4] with hyperedges {1, 2, 3} and {2, 3, 4} has 

XH = 2 F{1} + 6 F{2} + 2 F{3} + 4 F{1,2} + 8 F{1,3} + 4 F{2,3} − 2F{1,2,3},

and that the hypergraph with hyperedges {1, 2, 3}, {1, 4}, {2, 4}, {3, 4, 5} is not F -positive either, although distinct hyperedges of it meet in at most one vertex. Both of these contain cycles. On the 88 Preprint other hand Taylor (2015) proves that XH is F -positive whenever H is a hypertree each of whose hyperedges has prime size, and in that case obtains the explicit expansion XH = P 

> π

F (n)Des H (π) over all n! bijections π : V → [n], where the H-descents Des H (π) are read off from the unique path between consecutively labeled vertices. Taylor (2015, Conjecture A) asks for the removal of the primality hypothesis: 

Let H be a hypertree. Then XH is F -positive. 

The role of primality is isolated by Taylor (2015). Call an integer r ≥ 2 splittable if the nonconstant colorings of an r-element set can be partitioned into r! blocks indexed by the linear orders of that set, the block of a linear order consisting of the colorings that are weakly increasing along it and strictly increasing at some prescribed set of steps; he proves that XH is F -positive for every hypertree all of whose hyperedge sizes are splittable. He shows that every prime is splittable, by the cyclic standardization of Gessel & Reutenauer (1993), and reports a splitting for r = 4 found by computer search; he also observes that splittability of r is equivalent to partitionability of the Coxeter complex of type Ar−1 with the empty face removed, a scheduling problem in the sense of Breuer & Klivans (2016). Conjecture A was therefore known for every hypertree whose hyperedge sizes are all prime or equal to 4, and in particular for every hypertree with no hyperedge on more than 5 vertices. Each further composite size requires a splitting of its own. In a different direction, Pawlowski (2018) proves that XH is Schur-positive, hence F -positive, for every hyperforest whose line graph is bipartite, which settles Conjecture B of Taylor (2015); this does not reach all hypertrees, since three 6-element hyperedges through a common vertex form a hypertree whose line graph is K3 and whose hyperedge sizes are neither prime nor 4.

Theorem C.14.1. Let H = ( V, E ) be a finite hypertree and n = |V |. Then

This is Conjecture A of Taylor (2015). Every bijection V → [n] is a proper coloring, and the coefficient of x1x2 · · · xn in F (n)

n! fundamental quasisymmetric functions counted with multiplicity. Certain cases are immediate. If n = 1 then E = ∅, since every hyperedge has at least two vertices, and XH = x1 + x2 + · · · = F (1)  

> ∅

. We assume n ≥ 2 from now on, so that every vertex of H lies in a hyperedge. We now summarize the proof. Fix a hyperedge e of size r and, for a linear order τ of e and a set 

B ⊆ [r − 1] , consider the colorings of e that are weakly increasing along τ and strictly increas-ing at the steps in B. The key point is that the indicator function of “ κ is nonconstant on e” is a nonnegative rational combination of the indicator functions of these conditions, taken over all r!

orders τ and all nonempty B, with weights depending only on r and B. The weights are forced by the fundamental expansion of the chromatic symmetric function of a single hyperedge, and their nonnegativity is the elementary fact that every subset of [r − 1] is the descent set of at least one permutation of [r]. Multiplying this local identity over the hyperedges expresses XH as a nonneg-ative rational combination of generating functions for systems of weak and strict inequalities, one inequality per consecutive pair inside each chosen order. The hypertree hypothesis makes each such system a (P, ω )-partition condition on an ordinary tree, so each of these generating functions is 

F -positive, and the fundamental coefficients of XH are nonnegative rationals. They are integers because the monomial quasisymmetric coefficients of XH are integers and the two bases are related by an integral unitriangular matrix. C.14.2 A WEIGHTED LOCAL DECOMPOSITION FOR ONE HYPEREDGE

Lemma C.14.6. Let H = ( V, E ) be a hypertree with |V | ≥ 2. Then its incidence graph is a tree, distinct hyperedges of H meet in at most one vertex, and 

X

> e∈E

(|e| − 1) = |V | − 1.

Proof. The incidence graph has vertex set V ⊔ E and an edge joining v to e whenever v ∈ e. Since 

|V | ≥ 2 and H is connected, every vertex of H lies in a hyperedge, and every path of H from v to 

v′ is a walk of the incidence graph from v to v′; as each hyperedge is adjacent to its own vertices, the incidence graph is connected. It is bipartite and simple, so each of its cycles has even length 2m

with m ≥ 2 and reads v1, e 1, v 2, e 2, . . . , v m, e m, v 1 with the vi distinct and the ei distinct, which is precisely a cycle of H. As H has no cycles the incidence graph is acyclic, hence a tree. A tree on |V | + |E| vertices has |V | + |E| − 1 edges, and the incidence graph has P 

> e∈E

|e| edges, so P 

> e∈E

|e| = |V | + |E| − 1, which is the displayed identity. Finally, if distinct hyperedges e, e ′ both contained distinct vertices u, v , then u, e, v, e ′, u would be a cycle of H.Given Ω, let TΩ be the graph on vertex set V whose edges join consecutive vertices in the chosen order of each hyperedge,

Lemma C.14.9. For every Ω one has KΩ = Γ( PΩ, ω Ω). In particular KΩ is a nonnegative integral combination of fundamental quasisymmetric functions. Proof. Suppose first that κ satisfies equation 36. Then along every oriented edge u → v of TΩ one has κ(u) ≤ κ(v), with strict inequality when the edge is strict. If x < PΩ y there is a directed path from x to y in TΩ, and chaining the inequalities along it gives κ(x) ≤ κ(y). Suppose in addition that 

ωΩ(x) > ω Ω(y). By equation 38 the value of ωΩ increases along every weak edge, so if all edges of that directed path were weak we would get ωΩ(x) < ω Ω(y). Hence the path contains a strict edge, so at least one of the chained inequalities is strict and κ(x) < κ (y). Thus κ is a (PΩ, ω Ω)-partition. Conversely, suppose κ is a (PΩ, ω Ω)-partition and let u → v be an oriented edge of TΩ, so that 

u < PΩ v. If the edge is weak then ωΩ(u) < ω Ω(v) by equation 38 and the definition gives κ(u) ≤

κ(v). If it is strict then ωΩ(u) > ω Ω(v) and the definition gives κ(u) < κ (v). Ranging over the edges contributed by a hyperedge e recovers exactly the conditions equation 36 for e.The colorings counted by KΩ are therefore precisely the (PΩ, ω Ω)-partitions, and the last assertion follows from equation 39. Remark C.14.10 . The weighted decomposition produces no splittings, and hence says nothing about the partitionability of the Coxeter complexes of type Ar−1 with the empty face removed. Nor does it recover the combinatorial interpretation of the coefficients that Taylor (2015) gives in the prime case. Theorem C.14.1 establishes that the aS are nonnegative integers, but the decomposition equation 37 realizes them only as nonnegative rational combinations of numbers of linear extensions of posets. 

Remark C.14.11 . Connectedness enters the argument only in Lemma C.14.6 and in the proof of Lemma C.14.7, where it supplies a path of H between any two vertices. For a hyperforest, that is, a disjoint union of hypertrees, the same construction produces a spanning forest TΩ whose compo-nents are the trees built inside the components of H, and PΩ is the disjoint union of the correspond-ing posets; equation 39 applies verbatim and Theorem C.14.1 holds for hyperforests as well. 93 Preprint

Chromatic symmetric functions of hypertrees. arXiv preprint arXiv:1506.08262 , 2015. C.15 QUARTICALLY MANY FANO SUBSQUARES IN LATIN SQUARES 

This problem asks whether a Latin square of order n can contain more than cubically many sub-squares of order 7. We show that it can: for every n ≥ 56 there is a Latin square of order n with more than (n/ 28) 4 subsquares isotopic to the Fano square S7. A quartic upper bound is already due to Browning et al. (2015), so it is the lower bound that settles the order of growth; we also prove the explicit upper bound n4 by a short self-contained argument. The construction is an affine lift of the Fano quasigroup over a finite field of characteristic 2. The upper bound comes from the observation that three rows and one column already generate the whole incidence structure of S7.C.15.1 INTRODUCTION 

Let M be a Latin square of order n. A subsquare of order m in M is a set of m rows together with a set of m columns whose induced m × m subarray contains only m distinct symbols; that subarray is then itself a Latin square, and its symbol set is determined by the chosen rows and columns. Two Latin squares are isotopic if one is carried to the other by relabeling rows, columns and symbols independently. Following Browning et al. (2014), write ζ(n, m ) for the largest number of subsquares of order m in a Latin square of order n, and, for a fixed Latin square L, write ζ∗(n, L )

for the largest number of subsquares isotopic to L in a Latin square of order n.Let V = F32 and P = V \ { 0}. The Fano square S7 is the Cayley table of the quasigroup (P, ◦)

defined by 

x ◦ y =

x, x = y, x + y, x̸ = y, (40) with addition in V . Equivalently, S7 is the Steiner quasigroup of the Fano plane: the unordered triples {x, y, x + y} with x̸ = y are exactly the seven lines of PG(2 , 2) . Wanless asked the following at the LOOPS ’11 open problem session (LOOPS ’11, 2011, Prob-lem 2.8). 

Fix a prime p. Is there a family of latin squares with more than cubically many subsquares of order 

p? More precisely, is it true that for every constant c there is a latin square L of order n such that there are more than cn 3 subsquares of order p in L?

The note accompanying the problem records that the answer is negative for p ∈ { 2, 3, 5}, and that for p = 7 the subsquares would have to be multiplication tables of a Steiner quasigroup. Since the Steiner triple system of order 7 is unique up to isomorphism, the only Steiner quasigroup of order 7

is (P, ◦). The case p = 7 of the problem is therefore precisely the question, left open by Browning et al. (2014), of whether ζ∗(n, S 7) grows faster than cubically. We answer it affirmatively. The results of Browning et al. (2014) give the exact order of growth of ζ(n, m ) for several small 

m: one has ζ(n, m ) = Θ( n3) for m ∈ { 2, 3, 5} and ζ(n, m ) = Θ( n4) for m ∈ { 4, 6, 9, 10 }, with the sharper bounds n3/8 + O(n2) ≤ ζ(n, 2) ≤ n3/4 + O(n2) and n3/27 + O(n5/2) ≤ ζ(n, 3) ≤

n3/18 + O(n2). For a fixed square they show that ζ∗(n, L ) = Θ( n3) when L is cyclic, that 

ζ∗(n, L ) = O(n3) for a large class of L, and that every L admits some ε ∈ (0 , 1) with ζ∗(n, L ) = Ω( n2+ ε). The value m = 7 is absent from both lists, and by the note quoted above the only order-7

isotopy class that can push ζ(n, 7) past cubic growth is S7. On the upper bound side, Browning et al. (2015, Theorem 8) show that a Latin square of order n has O(nψ(m,t )+ t) subsquares of order 

m for all positive integers t ≤ m ≤ n, where ψ(m, t ) = ⌈ 12 ⌊m/t ⌋⌉ for odd m; with m = 7 and 

t = 2 this gives ζ(n, 7) = O(n4). For subsquares of unbounded order, Browning et al. (2013) prove that a Latin square of order n has at most nO(log k) subsquares of order k.Extremal behavior here is very far from typical behavior. McKay & Wanless (1999) show that for every ε > 0 almost all Latin squares of order n have at least n3/2−ε subsquares of order 2,and Allsop & Wanless (2025) show that a uniformly random k × n Latin rectangle has no proper subsquare of order 4 or more with probability 1 − O(1 /n ). In particular a random Latin square of order n has, with probability 1 − O(1 /n ), no proper subsquare of order 7, so a positive answer must come from an explicit construction. The main result of this section is the following. 

Theorem C.15.1. For every n ≥ 56 ,

 n

28 

4

< ζ ∗(n, S 7) ≤ n4.

In particular ζ∗(n, S 7) = Θ( n4).

Combined with the bound of Browning et al. (2015) quoted above, this settles the order of growth of ζ(n, 7) as well, adding 7 to the list of m for which ζ(n, m ) is known. 

Corollary C.15.2. ζ(n, 7) = Θ( n4).

We now summarize the construction. The key point is that S7 is almost an F2-linear object: off the diagonal the rule equation 40 is simply addition in V , and the diagonal rule x ◦ x = x is the only obstruction to linearity. We therefore work on P × Fq with q a power of two, keep the additive rule off the diagonal, and replace the diagonal rule by a nontrivial affine combination λu + (1 + λ)v in the fiber coordinate. The copies of S7 are then the translated graphs of the F2-linear maps V → Fq :there are q3 such maps and q translations, giving q4 subsquares in a Latin square of order 7q. The affine parameter λ is what forces the diagonal cells of each graph to close up correctly. 95 Preprint C.15.2 THE CONSTRUCTION 

Fix a power of two q ≥ 4 and an element λ ∈ Fq \ { 0, 1}. Define a binary operation ∗ on P × Fq by 

(x, u ) ∗ (y, v ) = 

(x, λu + (1 + λ)v), x = y, 

(x + y, u + v), x̸ = y, (41) where the first coordinate is computed in V and the second in Fq . Write Lq for the resulting array, with rows and columns indexed by P × Fq .

Lemma C.15.3. Lq is a Latin square of order 7q.Proof. Since |P × Fq | = 7 q, it suffices to check that ∗ is a quasigroup operation, that is, that for each row and each symbol there is a unique column producing that symbol, and likewise with the roles of rows and columns interchanged. Fix a row (x, u ) and a symbol (z, w ), and seek a column (y, v ) with (x, u )∗(y, v ) = ( z, w ). Suppose first that z = x. The second branch of equation 41 would force x + y = z = x, hence y = 0 /∈ P ,so the first branch applies and y = x. The remaining equation λu + (1 + λ)v = w has the unique solution 

v = (1 + λ)−1(w + λu ),

because 1 + λ̸ = 0 . Suppose next that z̸ = x. The first branch would force z = x, so the second branch applies and y = x + z, which is nonzero and distinct from x because z̸ = x and z̸ = 0 . The remaining equation u + v = w has the unique solution v = w + u.Now fix a column (y, v ) and a symbol (z, w ), and seek a row (x, u ). If z = y then exactly as before 

x = y, and λu + (1 + λ)v = w has the unique solution u = λ−1(w + (1 + λ)v) because λ̸ = 0 . If 

z̸ = y then x = y + z ∈ P with x̸ = y, and u = w + v.We next exhibit many Fano subsquares of Lq . Let ϕ : V → Fq be an F2-linear map and let r ∈ Fq .Put 

˜r = λ−1(1 + λ)r, 

and define 

Rϕ,r = {(x, ϕ (x) + r) : x ∈ P }, Cϕ,r = {(x, ϕ (x) + ˜ r) : x ∈ P },Tϕ,r = {(x, ϕ (x) + r + ˜ r) : x ∈ P }.

Each of these sets has exactly seven elements, since its members have distinct first coordinates. 

Lemma C.15.4. For every pair (ϕ, r ) the subarray of Lq on the rows Rϕ,r and the columns Cϕ,r is a subsquare with symbol set Tϕ,r , and it is isotopic to S7.Proof. Index the row (x, ϕ (x) + r), the column (y, ϕ (y) + ˜ r) and the symbol (z, ϕ (z) + r + ˜ r) by their first coordinates x, y, z ∈ P . We claim that the row indexed by x times the column indexed by 

y is the symbol indexed by x ◦ y.Suppose x̸ = y. The second branch of equation 41 applies, and since ϕ is F2-linear, 

(ϕ(x) + r) + ( ϕ(y) + ˜ r) = ϕ(x + y) + r + ˜ r. 

The product is therefore the symbol indexed by x + y = x ◦ y.Suppose instead x = y. The first branch of equation 41 applies, and in characteristic 2,

λ ϕ(x) + r + (1 + λ) ϕ(x) + ˜ r = ( λ + 1 + λ)ϕ(x) + λr + (1 + λ)˜ r = ϕ(x) + λr + (1 + λ)˜ r. 

The definition of ˜r says exactly that λ˜r = (1 + λ)r, and adding λr + ˜ r to both sides of this identity turns it into 

λr + (1 + λ)˜ r = r + ˜ r. 

The product is therefore the symbol indexed by x = x ◦ x, which proves the claim. Consequently the subarray on Rϕ,r ×Cϕ,r uses exactly the seven symbols of Tϕ,r , so it is a subsquare of order 7, and the three indexings by elements of P exhibit an isotopism from S7 onto it. 96 Preprint 

Lemma C.15.5. The q4 subsquares produced by Lemma C.15.4 are pairwise distinct. Proof. There are q3 F2-linear maps V → Fq and q choices of r, so there are q4 pairs (ϕ, r ); it suffices to show that distinct pairs give distinct row sets. Suppose Rϕ,r = Rψ,s . Each of the two sets contains exactly one element with first coordinate x, for every x ∈ P , so ϕ(x) + r = ψ(x) + s

for every x ∈ P . Thus the F2-linear map h = ϕ + ψ takes the constant value r + s on P . Choosing linearly independent a, b ∈ V gives 

r + s = h(a + b) = h(a) + h(b) = ( r + s) + ( r + s) = 0 .

Hence r = s, and h vanishes on P and at 0, so ϕ = ψ.

Corollary C.15.6. For every power of two q ≥ 4 one has ζ∗(7 q, S 7) ≥ q4. Moreover ζ∗(n, S 7) >

(n/ 28) 4 for every n ≥ 56 .Proof. The first assertion is immediate from Lemmas C.15.3, C.15.4 and C.15.5. For the second, let n ≥ 56 and let q be the largest power of two with 14 q ≤ n. Then q ≥ 4, and maximality gives 28 q > n , so q > n/ 28 . By Evans’ embedding theorem, every partial Latin square of order N embeds in a Latin square of every order at least 2N (Evans, 1960). Applying this to Lq ,which is a Latin square of order 7q and in particular a partial Latin square of order 7q, produces a Latin square M of order n ≥ 14 q whose subarray on the first 7q rows and columns is Lq . Every subsquare of Lq is a subsquare of M , so M contains at least q4 > (n/ 28) 4 subsquares isotopic to 

S7.

Example C.15.7 . Take q = 4 , so that F4 = {0, 1, α, α 2} with α3 = 1 , and λ ∈ { α, α 2}. Then L4 is a Latin square of order 28 containing at least 44 = 256 subsquares isotopic to S7.C.15.3 THE UPPER BOUND 

It is convenient to view a Latin square M of order n as a tripartite incidence structure. Its vertices are the n rows, the n columns and the n symbols of M , and each of the n2 cells contributes the triple consisting of its row, its column and the symbol it carries. The defining property of a Latin square is exactly that any two vertices lying in different parts belong to a unique common triple. Isotopisms are precisely the isomorphisms of these structures that respect the three parts. Observe that if K is a subsquare of M and two of the three vertices of some triple of M belong to 

K, then so does the third. Indeed, if a row and a column of K are given then the symbol in that cell is a symbol of K; if a row and a symbol of K are given then the column in which that symbol occurs in that row of K is the unique such column in M ; and the third case is symmetric. For x ∈ P write Rx, Cx and Tx for the row, column and symbol vertices of S7 indexed by x, so that 

Rx, Cy and Tx◦y form a triple for all x, y ∈ P .

Lemma C.15.8. Let e1, e 2, e 3 be a basis of V = F32 and put e4 = e1 + e2 + e3. Then the four vertices Re1 , R e2 , R e3 , C e4 generate all twenty-one vertices of S7 under the rule that two known vertices in different parts determine the third vertex of their triple. Proof. Throughout we use equation 40; note that ei̸ = e4 for i ∈ { 1, 2, 3}, since e4 = ei would force the sum of the other two basis vectors to vanish. Pairing each of Re1 , R e2 , R e3 with Ce4 produces 

Te2+e3 , Te1+e3 , Te1+e2 ,

because ei ◦ e4 = ei + e4. Next, if x ∈ P and x ◦ y = w with w̸ = x, then y̸ = x and y = x + w is determined. Applying this three times, 

Re1 , T e1+e3 give Ce3 , Re1 , T e1+e2 give Ce2 , Re2 , T e1+e2 give Ce1 .

The pairs Rei , C ei then give Tei for i ∈ { 1, 2, 3}, since ei ◦ ei = ei. Applying the same rule as before, 

Re1 , T e2 give Ce1+e2 , Re1 , T e3 give Ce1+e3 , Re2 , T e3 give Ce2+e3 .

97 Preprint At this stage the columns 

Ce1 , C e2 , C e3 , C e1+e2 , C e1+e3 , C e2+e3 , C e4

are all known, and these are all seven columns of S7. Pairing Re1 with each of them yields the symbols Te1◦y for y ∈ P ; as y runs over P the element e1 ◦ y runs over all of P , so every symbol vertex is known. Pairing Ce1 with each symbol vertex then yields every row vertex, since for each 

z ∈ P there is a unique x ∈ P with x ◦ e1 = z.

Corollary C.15.9. For every n one has ζ∗(n, S 7) ≤ n4.Proof. Let M be a Latin square of order n and let K be a subsquare of M isotopic to S7, and let e1, e 2, e 3, e 4 be as in Lemma C.15.8. Choose an isotopism θ from S7 onto K and record the quadruple  θ(Re1 ), θ (Re2 ), θ (Re3 ), θ (Ce4 ),

which consists of three rows and one column of M . There are at most n4 such quadruples, so it suffices to show that the quadruple determines K.Since θ is an isotopism onto K, it carries triples of S7 to triples of M that lie in K. By the observation above, applying the rule “two vertices in different parts determine the third vertex of their triple” inside M to vertices of K never leaves K, and it agrees with the corresponding rule in S7 transported by θ. By Lemma C.15.8, starting from the recorded quadruple and iterating this rule inside M

therefore produces exactly the twenty-one vertices of K. Hence K is determined by the quadruple, as desired. C.15.4 PROOF OF THE MAIN THEOREM 

Proof of Theorem C.15.1. Corollary C.15.6 gives ζ∗(n, S 7) > (n/ 28) 4 for every n ≥ 56 , and Corollary C.15.9 gives ζ∗(n, S 7) ≤ n4 for every n.

Remark C.15.10 . Along the sequence n = 7 q with q a power of two, Corollary C.15.6 gives the stronger bound ζ∗(n, S 7) ≥ (n/ 7) 4, so that

n4 ≤ 1.

The correct constant remains undetermined. 

Proof of Corollary C.15.2. Every subsquare isotopic to S7 has order 7, so ζ(n, 7) ≥ ζ∗(n, S 7) = Ω( n4) by Theorem C.15.1. In the other direction, the bound of Browning et al. (2015, Theorem 8) with m = 7 and t = 2 gives ζ(n, 7) = O(n4).
